People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi... yeah. I came in because, well, my mind just keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like, I'll be out there on the road—I'm a flagger—and I'll look at the road-closure forms or even just be holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I cannot remember the steps I have done for years. It's like my brain just completely empties out. 

Which is dangerous out there, you know? The drivers are already so distracted. Just Tuesday this huge red pickup truck almost clipped one of my cones. It actually reminded me of my brother's old Chevy. He loved that truck, but he had to sell it when gas prices went up because he was driving all the way to the city for his landscaping job. He lives in Colorado now, though. It's really beautiful out there, lots of mountains, he sends me pictures of the snow... 

Wait, sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, my job. Yeah, I just look at the paddle and suddenly have no idea what I'm supposed to do.

It happens even when I'm completely calm. Like, there doesn't have to be any traffic at all. It started... gosh, it's been about a year and eight months now. Yeah, right around then. It really all started just after my closest friend died. We were both history majors, which is kind of funny because obviously I'm out on the road directing traffic now, but back then we thought we were going to be archivists or work in some big museum together. 

I always loved the idea of working in a museum. The lighting is always so dim and peaceful, and old paper has this very specific smell to it. It smells almost like vanilla and dust, you know? I read an article once that said it's the chemical compounds in the paper breaking down over time. Lignin, I think it's called. It's the same stuff that makes tree trunks rigid. Trees are amazing like that, especially the way their roots communicate underground through those vast networks of fungi. It's like they have their own secret language beneath the soil that we just walk right over every day without even realizing it...

No, I don't think so. I mean, my coworkers haven't said anything about me staring off or doing anything weird. They just see me standing there looking at my paddle. It's really just the blankness inside my head, you know? Like I'm just frozen trying to remember.

Well, yeah... most days are just really hard now. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness right in my chest. It's like... like the day is already completely ruined before I even put my feet on the floor. It's just this awful weight. 

My cat actually sleeps at the foot of my bed, right where my feet go. He's this big orange tabby named Marmalade. I adopted him from the shelter about five years ago, and he has this funny little quirk where he won't eat his dry food unless I put a tiny splash of warm water on it. The volunteer at the shelter told me he had some dental work done before I got him, so maybe his teeth are sensitive. Dental work is so expensive, isn't it? I remember when I had to get a root canal back in my twenties, the dentist had this poster on the ceiling of a hot air balloon over a canyon. I guess it was supposed to be relaxing, to look up at the balloon while they were drilling. Hot air balloons always seem so peaceful, just floating up there with the clouds, completely detached from everything on the ground...

Um... I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, just really down. Just that heavy feeling every morning.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I definitely have. I used to be so involved in things. I was always the one organizing rides for the community choir, or setting up the neighborhood garden swaps... oh, and I used to do all the snack schedules for the youth basketball league, too. I loved doing that. 

But now... I don't know, I just don't have the drive for any of it anymore. I can sit on my couch for hours just staring at my phone, seeing messages come in, and I just don't feel any push to answer anybody. It's like my phone is just this heavy block of glass. 

Glass is actually really fascinating when you think about it. It's technically an amorphous solid, which means it's not quite a liquid and not quite a solid. I watched a documentary once about how they make those beautiful stained glass windows in old cathedrals. They have to heat the sand up to such incredibly high temperatures, and then they blow it into these huge cylinders before they flatten it out. The colors come from adding different metal oxides, like cobalt for blue or gold for red. It's amazing that something so fragile can last for hundreds of years, just sitting there letting the light shine through... 

I'm sorry, I drifted off again. What were we talking about? Oh, hobbies. Yeah, I just don't do any of those things anymore. I just sit and stare at my phone.

Yeah, it's... it's really everywhere now, not just at work. I came in originally because my mind just keeps going blank at the absolute worst times. Like, I'll be looking at the road-closure forms, or even just holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I cannot remember the steps I've done for years. It's terrifying. 

And it happens with reading, too. I used to read a lot. I had this whole collection of mystery novels, the ones with the really intricate covers. I actually bought a special bookshelf for them from this antique store downtown. The guy who owned the store was so nice, he helped me tie it to the roof of my car because it wouldn't fit in the trunk. He used this special kind of knot, a trucker's hitch, I think he called it. He said he learned it when he was in the Navy. The Navy has so many different knots for everything, it's almost like its own language. I wonder how long it takes to learn all of them, or if you just forget the ones you don't use... 

Wait, sorry. I'm doing it again. You were asking about concentrating? Yeah, it's just really hard. The blankness just happens with everything, even just looking at my forms or my paddle. I just can't hold onto a thought.

My sleep? Well, I don't know... I guess I haven't really noticed a big change in how much I'm sleeping. But my energy... I just feel so drained all the time. Just completely exhausted, even if I haven't done anything. 

It's funny, speaking of energy, I was thinking about how much energy it takes to power those big traffic lights at the intersections. I used to stand under one every day before they moved me to the construction sites. They look so small when you're driving under them, but if you get up close, they're actually huge. I saw a crew replacing one once, and they had to use this massive cherry picker just to lift it. The guy in the bucket had on this bright neon yellow harness. Neon colors are so interesting, how they catch the light. I think it has something to do with the way the dye absorbs ultraviolet light and then re-emits it as visible light. It's like the material itself is glowing. I bought a neon pink highlighter once to mark up my schedules, but it was so bright it actually gave me a headache...

I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, my sleep. Yeah, I'm not really sure if it's more or less, honestly. I just know I'm always tired.

I... yeah, I have. Since she died, I just keep replaying every single little thing I failed to do for her. I should have been there more. And it's not just her... it's the choir, my family... I just feel like I'm a completely useless person. Like I deserve whatever punishment is coming my way. 

Punishment is a strange concept, isn't it? I remember reading about how different cultures handled it throughout history. Like in the Middle Ages, they had those stocks in the town square. People would just throw rotten vegetables at you. I always wondered where they got the vegetables, like, did they bring them from home specifically for that, or was there a vendor nearby selling spoiled cabbages? Cabbage is actually really good for you, it's full of vitamin K and C. I used to make this amazing coleslaw for the neighborhood block parties. I'd use purple cabbage because it made the bowl look so vibrant. Purple is such a royal color. They used to make the dye out of crushed sea snails, thousands of them just for one cloak...

I... yeah, I have. I just don't have the energy to fight the thought anymore that it might be better if I just didn't wake up. It's just this heavy feeling, like a blanket. Blankets can be so heavy, especially those weighted ones. My sister bought me one of those weighted blankets once for Christmas. It was supposed to help with anxiety, filled with these tiny little glass beads. I always wondered how they got the beads so small and perfectly round without them shattering. Glassmaking is such an ancient art, isn't it? I saw a documentary once about Venetian glassblowers, how they spin the molten glass on those long metal tubes. The heat in those workshops must be absolutely unbearable, especially in the summer. I went to Venice once, years ago, right after college. The canals smelled a bit, to be honest, but the gondolas were beautiful. They paint them black because of some old sumptuary law from the 16th century to stop people from showing off their wealth...

I'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right... not waking up. Yeah, it scares my family because I've actually started saying it out loud lately. I just... I'm so tired of fighting it.

My mom actually used to have this huge green chalkboard in her classroom. She taught third grade. I used to go in after school and help her clap the erasers outside against the brick wall. You know, where you bang them together and all the white dust goes everywhere? It would get all over my shoes and my jeans. You can't really do that anymore, schools all use those dry erase markers now. Or those electronic smartboards. Kids today probably don't even know what chalk dust smells like, which is kind of sad, really. I always liked that smell. \n\nBut um... wait. What did you ask me? Oh, right, about being under pressure. No, it's just... I can be totally fine, and then I look at the forms or the paddle and I just can't remember anything I'm supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing this happening, and has it been getting more frequent or severe over time?\n\nPatient: It started... gosh, it's been about a year and eight months now. Yeah, right around then. It really all started just after my closest friend died. We actually met back when I was getting my bachelor's degree. We were both history majors, which is kind of funny because obviously I'm out on the road directing traffic now, but back then we thought we were going to be archivists or work in some big museum together. \n\nI always loved the idea of working in a museum. The lighting is always so dim and peaceful, and old paper has this very specific smell to it. It smells almost like vanilla and dust, you know? I read an article once that said it's the chemical compounds in the paper breaking down over time. Lignin, I think it's called. It's the same stuff that makes tree trunks rigid. Trees are amazing like that, especially the way their roots communicate underground through those vast networks of fungi. It's like they have their own secret language beneath the soil that we just walk right over every day without even realizing it... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I completely lost my train of thought again. What did you ask me? I know I said it's been a year and eight months since she passed, but did you ask me something else?\n\nDoctor: During these moments when your mind goes blank, have people around you noticed you staring off, making unusual movements, or being unresponsive when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my coworkers haven't said anything about me staring off or doing anything weird. They just see me standing there looking at my paddle. It's really just the blankness inside my head, you know? Like I'm just frozen trying to remember. \n\nIt reminds me of when my computer froze up last week. I was trying to order some new work boots online because my current ones have this hole right near the pinky toe. It's not a big hole, but when it rains, the water seeps right in and my sock gets soaked. There is nothing worse than wearing wet socks for an entire shift. It makes your feet so cold, and then the skin gets all wrinkly like you've been in the bathtub too long. I actually used to love taking long baths when I was little. My grandma would put this lavender bubble bath in the water, and she had these little plastic boats I would play with. One was a tugboat, and it was bright yellow...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I lost track. You were asking about my coworkers? Yeah, no, they haven't mentioned me doing anything strange or not responding. It's just the memory part that goes blank.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend passed away about a year and eight months ago, how would you describe your mood most days—have you been feeling down, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or noticing changes in your sleep or energy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... most days are just really hard now. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness right in my chest. It's like... like the day is already completely ruined before I even put my feet on the floor. It's just this awful weight. \n\nMy cat actually sleeps at the foot of my bed, right where my feet go. He's this big orange tabby named Marmalade. I adopted him from the shelter about five years ago, and he has this funny little quirk where he won't eat his dry food unless I put a tiny splash of warm water on it. The volunteer at the shelter told me he had some dental work done before I got him, so maybe his teeth are sensitive. Dental work is so expensive, isn't it? I remember when I had to get a root canal back in my twenties, the dentist had this poster on the ceiling of a hot air balloon over a canyon. I guess it was supposed to be relaxing, to look up at the balloon while they were drilling. Hot air balloons always seem so peaceful, just floating up there with the clouds, completely detached from everything on the ground...\n\nUm... I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, just really down. Just that heavy feeling every morning.\n\nDoctor: Besides the low mood in the mornings, have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like spending time with your cat or other hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I definitely have. I used to be so involved in things. I was always the one organizing rides for the community choir, or setting up the neighborhood garden swaps... oh, and I used to do all the snack schedules for the youth basketball league, too. I loved doing that. \n\nBut now... I don't know, I just don't have the drive for any of it anymore. I can sit on my couch for hours just staring at my phone, seeing messages come in, and I just don't feel any push to answer anybody. It's like my phone is just this heavy block of glass. \n\nGlass is actually really fascinating when you think about it. It's technically an amorphous solid, which means it's not quite a liquid and not quite a solid. I watched a documentary once about how they make those beautiful stained glass windows in old cathedrals. They have to heat the sand up to such incredibly high temperatures, and then they blow it into these huge cylinders before they flatten it out. The colors come from adding different metal oxides, like cobalt for blue or gold for red. It's amazing that something so fragile can last for hundreds of years, just sitting there letting the light shine through... \n\nI'm sorry, I drifted off again. What were we talking about? Oh, hobbies. Yeah, I just don't do any of those things anymore. I just sit and stare at my phone.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these blanking-out episodes at work, have you noticed general difficulty concentrating or focusing on conversations, reading, or other tasks in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's really everywhere now, not just at work. I came in originally because my mind just keeps going blank at the absolute worst times. Like, I'll be looking at the road-closure forms, or even just holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I cannot remember the steps I've done for years. It's terrifying. \n\nAnd it happens with reading, too. I used to read a lot. I had this whole collection of mystery novels, the ones with the really intricate covers. I actually bought a special bookshelf for them from this antique store downtown. The guy who owned the store was so nice, he helped me tie it to the roof of my car because it wouldn't fit in the trunk. He used this special kind of knot, a trucker's hitch, I think he called it. He said he learned it when he was in the Navy. The Navy has so many different knots for everything, it's almost like its own language. I wonder how long it takes to learn all of them, or if you just forget the ones you don't use... \n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. You were asking about concentrating? Yeah, it's just really hard. The blankness just happens with everything, even just looking at my forms or my paddle. I just can't hold onto a thought.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over these past months—are you sleeping more or less than usual, and how would you describe your energy levels during the day?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Well, I don't know... I guess I haven't really noticed a big change in how much I'm sleeping. But my energy... I just feel so drained all the time. Just completely exhausted, even if I haven't done anything. \n\nIt's funny, speaking of energy, I was thinking about how much energy it takes to power those big traffic lights at the intersections. I used to stand under one every day before they moved me to the construction sites. They look so small when you're driving under them, but if you get up close, they're actually huge. I saw a crew replacing one once, and they had to use this massive cherry picker just to lift it. The guy in the bucket had on this bright neon yellow harness. Neon colors are so interesting, how they catch the light. I think it has something to do with the way the dye absorbs ultraviolet light and then re-emits it as visible light. It's like the material itself is glowing. I bought a neon pink highlighter once to mark up my schedules, but it was so bright it actually gave me a headache...\n\nI'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, my sleep. Yeah, I'm not really sure if it's more or less, honestly. I just know I'm always tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness, excessive guilt, or feeling hopeless about the future since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I have. Since she died, I just keep replaying every single little thing I failed to do for her. I should have been there more. And it's not just her... it's the choir, my family... I just feel like I'm a completely useless person. Like I deserve whatever punishment is coming my way. \n\nPunishment is a strange concept, isn't it? I remember reading about how different cultures handled it throughout history. Like in the Middle Ages, they had those stocks in the town square. People would just throw rotten vegetables at you. I always wondered where they got the vegetables, like, did they bring them from home specifically for that, or was there a vendor nearby selling spoiled cabbages? Cabbage is actually really good for you, it's full of vitamin K and C. I used to make this amazing coleslaw for the neighborhood block parties. I'd use purple cabbage because it made the bowl look so vibrant. Purple is such a royal color. They used to make the dye out of crushed sea snails, thousands of them just for one cloak...\n\nWait, I'm sorry. I lost my train of thought again. You asked about feeling worthless? Yeah... I just feel like I've failed everyone, and I deserve to be punished for it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about deserving punishment for these failures, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I have. I just don't have the energy to fight the thought anymore that it might be better if I just didn't wake up. It's just this heavy feeling, like a blanket. Blankets can be so heavy, especially those weighted ones. My sister bought me one of those weighted blankets once for Christmas. It was supposed to help with anxiety, filled with these tiny little glass beads. I always wondered how they got the beads so small and perfectly round without them shattering. Glassmaking is such an ancient art, isn't it? I saw a documentary once about Venetian glassblowers, how they spin the molten glass on those long metal tubes. The heat in those workshops must be absolutely unbearable, especially in the summer. I went to Venice once, years ago, right after college. The canals smelled a bit, to be honest, but the gondolas were beautiful. They paint them black because of some old sumptuary law from the 16th century to stop people from showing off their wealth...\n\nI'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right... not waking up. I... no, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I just... I don't have the energy to fight the thought anymore that it might be better if I just didn't wake up. And that scares my family because I've started saying it out loud to them. It just slips out. 

Slips out... like when you're on the ice. We had to salt the roads so much this past winter at the construction site. Rock salt is actually terrible for the concrete, it causes it to pit and crack over time. They have these new chemical de-icers now, potassium acetate I think, that are supposed to be better for the environment and the roads. The environment is just taking such a beating, isn't it? I saw this article about how the polar bears are migrating further south because the sea ice is melting, and they're actually breeding with grizzly bears now. They call them pizzly bears or grolar bears. Can you imagine running into one of those? Grizzlies are already so aggressive, and polar bears actively hunt humans. Bears are fascinating, though. The way they can just slow their heart rate down to almost nothing during hibernation... just sleeping through the worst parts of the year...

I'm sorry. I did it again, didn't I? You were asking if I had a plan. No, no plan. I just... I say I want to go to sleep and not wake up, and it really upsets my sister when I say it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi... yeah. I came in because, well, my mind just keeps going blank at the worst possible times. Like, I'll be out there on the road—I'm a flagger—and I'll look at the road-closure forms or even just be holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I cannot remember the steps I have done for years. It's just this awful weight. \n\nMy cat actually sleeps at the foot of my bed, right where my feet go. He's this big orange tabby named Marmalade. I adopted him from the shelter about five years ago, and he has this funny little quirk where he won't eat his dry food unless I put a tiny splash of warm water on it. The volunteer at the shelter told me he had some dental work done before I got him, so maybe his teeth are sensitive. Dental work is so expensive, isn't it? I remember when I had to get a root canal back in my twenties, the dentist had this poster on the ceiling of a hot air balloon over a canyon. I guess it was supposed to be relaxing, to look up at the balloon while they were drilling. Hot air balloons always seem so peaceful, just floating up there with the clouds, completely detached from everything on the ground...\n\nUm... I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, my mood. Yeah, just really down. I loved doing that. \n\nBut now... I don't know, I just don't have the drive for any of it anymore. I can sit on my couch for hours just staring at my phone, seeing messages come in, and I just don't feel any push to answer anybody. It's like my phone is just this heavy block of glass. \n\nGlass is actually really fascinating when you think about it. The colors come from adding different metal oxides, like cobalt for blue or gold for red. It's amazing that something so fragile can last for hundreds of years, just sitting there letting the light shine through... \n\nI'm sorry, I drifted off again. What were we talking about? Oh, hobbies. Yeah, I just don't do any of those things anymore. I just sit and stare at my phone.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these blanking-out episodes at work, have you noticed general difficulty concentrating or focusing on conversations, reading, or other tasks in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's really everywhere now, not just at work. I came in originally because my mind just keeps going blank at the absolute worst times. Like, I'll be looking at the road-closure forms, or even just holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I cannot remember the steps I've done for years. It's terrifying. \n\nAnd it happens with reading, too. I used to read a lot. I had this whole collection of mystery novels, the ones with the really intricate covers. I actually bought a special bookshelf for them from this antique store downtown. The guy who owned the store was so nice, he helped me tie it to the roof of my car because it wouldn't fit in the trunk. He used this special kind of knot, a trucker's hitch, I think he called it. He said he learned it when he was in the Navy. The Navy has so many different knots for everything, it's almost like its own language. I wonder how long it takes to learn all of them, or if you just forget the ones you don't use... \n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again. You were asking about concentrating? Yeah, it's just really hard. The blankness just happens with everything, even just looking at my forms or my paddle. I just can't hold onto a thought.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over these past months—are you sleeping more or less than usual, and how would you describe your energy levels during the day?\n\nPatient: My sleep? Well, I don't know... I guess I haven't really noticed a big change in how much I'm sleeping. But my energy... I just feel so drained all the time. Just completely exhausted, even if I haven't done anything. \n\nIt's funny, speaking of energy, I was thinking about how much energy it takes to power those big traffic lights at the intersections. I used to stand under one every day before they moved me to the construction sites. They look so small when you're driving under them, but if you get up close, they're actually huge. I saw a crew replacing one once, and they had to use this massive cherry picker just to lift it. The guy in the bucket had on this bright neon yellow harness. Neon colors are so interesting, how they catch the light. I think it has something to do with the way the dye absorbs ultraviolet light and then re-emits it as visible light. It's like the material itself is glowing. I bought a neon pink highlighter once to mark up my schedules, but it was so bright it actually gave me a headache...\n\nI'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, my sleep. Yeah, I'm not really sure if it's more or less, honestly. I just know I'm always tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of worthlessness, excessive guilt, or feeling hopeless about the future since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I have. Since she died, I just keep replaying every single little thing I failed to do for her. I should have been there more. And it's not just her... it's the choir, my family... I just feel like I'm a completely useless person. Like I deserve whatever punishment is coming my way. \n\nPunishment is a strange concept, isn't it? I remember reading about how different cultures handled it throughout history. Like in the Middle Ages, they had those stocks in the town square. People would just throw rotten vegetables at you. I always wondered where they got the vegetables, like, did they bring them from home specifically for that, or was there a vendor nearby selling spoiled cabbages? Cabbage is actually really good for you, it's full of vitamin K and C. I used to make this amazing coleslaw for the neighborhood block parties. I'd use purple cabbage because it made the bowl look so vibrant. Purple is such a royal color. They used to make the dye out of crushed sea snails, thousands of them just for one cloak...\n\nWait, I'm sorry. I lost my train of thought again. You asked about feeling worthless? Yeah... I just feel like I've failed everyone, and I deserve to be punished for it.\n\nDoctor: When you think about deserving punishment for these failures, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I have. I just don't have the energy to fight the thought anymore that it might be better if I just didn't wake up. It's just this heavy feeling, like a blanket. Blankets can be so heavy, especially those weighted ones. My sister bought me one of those weighted blankets once for Christmas. It was supposed to help with anxiety, filled with these tiny little glass beads. I always wondered how they got the beads so small and perfectly round without them shattering. Glassmaking is such an ancient art, isn't it? I saw a documentary once about Venetian glassblowers, how they spin the molten glass on those long metal tubes. The heat in those workshops must be absolutely unbearable, especially in the summer. I went to Venice once, years ago, right after college. The canals smelled a bit, to be honest, but the gondolas were beautiful. They paint them black because of some old sumptuary law from the 16th century to stop people from showing off their wealth...\n\nI'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right... not waking up. Yeah, it scares my family because I've actually started saying it out loud lately. I just... I'm so tired of fighting it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts of not waking up, have you thought about how you might do it, or do you have any intention of acting on these thoughts?\n\nPatient: I... no, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I just... I don't have the energy to fight the thought anymore that it might be better if I just didn't wake up. And that scares my family because I've started saying it out loud to them. It just slips out. \n\nSlips out... like when you're on the ice. We had to salt the roads so much this past winter at the construction site. The way they can just slow their heart rate down to almost nothing during hibernation... just sleeping through the worst parts of the year...\n\nI'm sorry. I did it again, didn't I? {"candidates": ["F32.2", "F33.2", "F43.21"], "note": "Patient presents with major depressive symptoms (depressed mood, anhedonia, worthlessness, guilt, fatigue, suicidal ideation without plan) emerging ~20 months after the death of a close friend, consistent with a major depressive episode (single episode F32.2, given no clear prior episodes mentioned, though recurrent F33.2 remains possible pending history). The cognitive 'blanking out' and marked circumstantial/tangential speech with derailment likely reflect severe concentration difficulty and psychomotor/cognitive slowing from depression rather than a primary thought disorder, though the degree of tangentiality raises some question of an underlying cognitive or attentional disturbance to monitor. Prolonged grief disorder (F43.21) remains a differential given the temporal link to bereavement, though the pervasive neurovegetative and cognitive symptoms suggest depression has developed beyond normal grief.", "is_final": false}

I... I don't really know. I mean, I've had times where I felt down, but I don't think it was like this. Not for two weeks straight, anyway. It's hard to keep track of time, honestly. 

Time is such a weird thing, isn't it? When I'm out there flagging traffic, sometimes an hour feels like a whole day. You just stand there with the stop sign, and the cars just keep coming. Some people get so angry when you make them stop. This one guy in a red pickup truck used to yell at me every single morning. Red cars actually get pulled over more often, did you know that? It's an optical illusion, they look like they're going faster than they actually are. I read a study about how insurance companies used to charge more for red cars, but they don't really do that anymore. Insurance is such a scam anyway. You pay all this money for years, and then when something actually happens, they try everything they can to get out of paying. My sister had a pipe burst in her basement last year, and they claimed it was "wear and tear" so they wouldn't cover the water damage. Water damage is the worst, it gets into the drywall and then the mold starts... black mold is so toxic, it can cause all sorts of respiratory issues...

I'm sorry, I forgot what we were talking about. Did I answer your question? About feeling depressed before? I don't... I don't think I've ever felt this bad for this long before she died.

The volunteer at the shelter told me he had some dental work done before I got him, so maybe his teeth are sensitive. Dental work is so expensive, isn't it? I remember when I had to get a root canal back in my twenties, the dentist had this poster on the ceiling of a hot air balloon over a canyon. I loved doing that. \n\nBut now... I don't know, I just don't have the drive for any of it anymore. I can sit on my couch for hours just staring at my phone, seeing messages come in, and I just don't feel any push to answer anybody. It's like my phone is just this heavy block of glass. \n\nGlass is actually really fascinating when you think about it. It's technically an amorphous solid, which means it's not quite a liquid and not quite a solid. I watched a documentary once about how they make those beautiful stained glass windows in old cathedrals. They have to heat the sand up to such incredibly high temperatures, and then they blow it into these huge cylinders before they flatten it out. The colors come from adding different metal oxides, like cobalt for blue or gold for red. It's amazing that something so fragile can last for hundreds of years, just sitting there letting the light shine through... \n\nI'm sorry, I drifted off again. What were we talking about? Like, I'll be looking at the road-closure forms, or even just holding my stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I cannot remember the steps I've done for years.
